j:\widetilde{\mathcal{F}\ell}_{w}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{F}\ell}_{\overline{w}}^{s}
,
i:\widetilde{\mathcal{F}\ell}_{ws}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{F}\ell}_{\overline{w}}^{s}
, and
h:\widetilde{\mathcal{F}\ell}_{\overline{w}}^{s}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{F}\ell}
denote the inclusion maps.
As discussed in the proof of Lemma
3.3.6
, there are isomorphisms
j^{*}\mathcal{K}_{w,ws}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\mathcal{K}_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}(\ell(w))
and
i^{*}\mathcal{K}_{w,ws}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\mathcal{K}_{ws}^{\mathcal{L}}(\ell(w))
.
Note that here
j^{*}
and
i^{*}
can be safely interpreted as restrictions in the non-mixed category since they are restrictions onto single strata, and hence, they
preserve parity.
If we apply the functorial distinguished triangle
h_{!}j_{!}j^{*}\to h_{!}\to h_{!}i_{!}i^{*}\to
to
\mathcal{K}_{w,ws}^{\mathcal{L}}(\ell(w))
, we obtain a distinguished triangle

(3.9.2)

\underline{\Delta}_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}\to\pi_{s}^{*}\underline{\Delta}_{\overline{w}}^{\mathcal{L}}(1)\to\underline{\Delta}_{ws}^{\mathcal{L}}(1)\to.

By appropriately rescaling the morphisms in (
3.9.2
), we obtain the desired distinguished triangle (c.f.,
[
AR16
, Lemma 4.1]
on why this can be done).
∎

The following is a mixed analogue of Proposition
3.3.10
.

Proposition 3.9.6
.

We have natural isomorphisms

(1)

\underline{\Delta}_{xy}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\underline{\Delta}_{x}^{y\mathcal{L}}\star\underline{\Delta}_{y}^{\mathcal{L}}
if
\ell(xy)=\ell(x)+\ell(y)
;

(2)

\underline{\nabla}_{xy}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\underline{\nabla}_{x}^{y\mathcal{L}}\star\underline{\nabla}_{y}^{\mathcal{L}}
if
\ell(xy)=\ell(x)+\ell(y)
;

(3)

\underline{\nabla}_{x^{-1}}^{x\mathcal{L}}\star\underline{\Delta}_{x}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\underline{\Delta}_{e}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\underline{\Delta}_{x^{-1}}^{x\mathcal{L}}\star\underline{\nabla}_{x}^{\mathcal{L}}
.

Proof.

By Verdier duality, we can reduce to proving just (1) and the first isomorphism in (3).
We can further reduce to the case where
y=s
is a simple reflection in (1) and
x=s
a simple reflection in (2).
If
s\notin W_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}
, both isomorphisms follow from Lemma
3.9.4
.
As a result, we will assume that
s\in W_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}
.
By the description of
\underline{\Delta}_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}
given in (
3.9.1
), there is a distinguished triangle

(3.9.3)

\underline{\Delta}_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\to\mathcal{E}_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\to\underline{\Delta}_{e}^{\mathcal{L}}(1).

We can now prove (1).
Apply
\Delta_{x}^{\mathcal{L}}\star(-)
to (
3.9.3
) to obtain a distinguished triangle

\underline{\Delta}_{x}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\underline{\Delta}_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\to\underline{\Delta}_{x}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\mathcal{E}_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\to\underline{\Delta}_{x}^{\mathcal{L}}(1).

By Lemma
3.3.5
and Lemma
3.9.5
(1), this distinguished triangle is of the form given in Lemma
3.9.5
(2).
Therefore, there is an isomorphism
\underline{\Delta}_{x}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\underline{\Delta}_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\underline{\Delta}_{xs}^{\mathcal{L}}
.

For (3), we apply
\underline{\nabla}_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}(-1)\star(-)
to (
3.9.3
) to obtain a distinguished triangle

(3.9.4)

\underline{\nabla}_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\underline{\Delta}_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}(-1)\to\underline{\nabla}_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\mathcal{E}_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}(-1)\to\underline{\nabla}_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}.

By a Verdier dual version of Lemma
3.9.2
(1) and Lemma
3.3.5
, we have an isomorphism
\underline{\nabla}_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\mathcal{E}_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}(-1)\cong\pi_{s}^{*}\underline{\nabla}_{\overline{s}}^{s,\mathcal{L}}(1)
.
It can be readily checked that
\underline{\nabla}_{\overline{s}}^{s,\mathcal{L}}\cong\IC_{\overline{s}}^{s,\mathcal{L}}
since
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}\ell}_{\overline{s}}^{s}
is closed in
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}\ell}
.
This observation combined with Lemma
3.2.8
yields an isomorphism
\underline{\nabla}_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\mathcal{E}_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}(-1)\cong\mathcal{E}_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}
. We can then
rewrite (
3.9.4
) as follows:

\underline{\nabla}_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\underline{\Delta}_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}(-1)\to\mathcal{E}_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\to\underline{\nabla}_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}.

This triangle is Verdier dual to (
3.9.3
) (after switching
\mathcal{L}
with
\mathcal{L}^{-1}
).
As a result, we obtain an isomorphism
\underline{\Delta}_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\underline{\nabla}_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\underline{\Delta}_{e}^{\mathcal{L}}
.
∎

Corollary 3.9.7
.

Let
\underline{w}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{k})
be a reduced expression of endosimple reflections for
w\in W_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}
. Then there are isomorphisms

\underline{\Delta}_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\underline{\Delta}_{s_{1}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\ldots\star\underline{\Delta}_{s_{k}}^{\mathcal{L}}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\underline{\nabla}_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\underline{\nabla}_{s_{1}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\ldots\star\underline{\nabla}_{s_{k}}^{\mathcal{L}}.

Proof.

The statement follows from a routine inductive argument on
\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w)
using Proposition
3.9.6
and Lemma
3.9.4
.
∎

3.9.8.
Right Equivariant Category

Consider the mixed derived category,

\mathcal{D}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{m}(\mathbbm{k})\coloneq K^{b}\textnormal{Par}_{/\mathcal{L}}(\mathbbm{k}).

For all
w\in W
, we can define the
standard
and
costandard
sheaves

\underline{\Delta}_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}\coloneq j_{w!}\mathcal{K}_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}(\ell(w))\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\underline{\nabla}_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}\coloneq j_{w*}\mathcal{K}_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}(\ell(w)).

The forgetful functor
{}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\backslash}\textnormal{For}:{}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\backslash}\textnormal{Par}_{/\mathcal{L}}(\mathbbm{k})\to\textnormal{Par}_{/\mathcal{L}}(\mathbbm{k})

induces a forgetful functor on mixed sheaves,

{}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\backslash}\textnormal{For}:{}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\backslash}\mathcal{D}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{m}(\mathbbm{k})\to\mathcal{D}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{m}(\mathbbm{k}).

It can be easily checked that
{}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\backslash}\textnormal{For}
commutes with
j_{w*}
and
j_{w!}
for any
w\in{}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}W_{\mathcal{L}}
(c.f.,
[
AR16
, (3.4)]
).
As a result, there are natural isomorphisms

(3.9.5)

{}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\backslash}\textnormal{For}(\underline{\Delta}_{w}^{\mathcal{L}})\cong\underline{\Delta}_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt{}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\backslash}\textnormal{For}(\underline{\nabla}_{w}^{\mathcal{L}})\cong\underline{\nabla}_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}

for all
w\in{}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}W_{\mathcal{L}}
.

4.
Soergel Theory for Parabolics of Finite Type

The main result of this section will give an equivalence between a parabolic version of the neutral block of the monodromic Hecke category of parity sheaves with
characteristic 0 coefficients and the category of Soergel bimodules associated to the endoscopic group.
This can be thought of as a simplified version of the more general neutral block endoscopic equivalence (Theorem
5.3.1
).
We restrict to finite parabolic subgroups of
W_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}
and to coefficients in a field of characteristic 0. These have the following benefits:

(1)

The finite conditions allow us to define the
\mathbb{H}
-functor using maximal IC sheaves,
\mathbb{H}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}:{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\textnormal{Par}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\Theta,\circ}(\mathbbm{k})\to\mathbb{S}\textnormal{Bim}_{W_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}
.

(2)

The characteristic 0 coefficients condition ensures that the
\mathbb{H}
-functor is an equivalence of monoidal categories.

We will later compare these parity sheaves with the Elias-Williamson diagrammatic Hecke category which will remove the above two restrictions.
Nonetheless, in the diagrammatic version, we will use the characteristic 0 Soergel theory in checking the defining relations of the diagrammatic Hecke category are as specified.

4.1.
Soergel Bimodules

We will provide a reminder on the general theory of Soergel bimodules.
For the most part, the ambient realization will be fixed, namely, it will be the Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
; however, the Coxeter group with change throughout.
As a result, we will provide some discussion on realizations.
All the results in this section are well-known, and as such, we will omit proofs.

4.1.1.
Realizations

Let
(W,S)
be a Coxeter system. Let
\mathbbm{k}
be a noetherian domain of finite global dimension.

Definition 4.1.2
.

A
realization
of
(W,S)
over
\mathbbm{k}
is a triple
(\mathfrak{h},\{\alpha_{s}\}_{s\in S},\{\alpha_{s}^{\vee}\}_{s\in S})
, where
\mathfrak{h}
is a finite rank free

\mathbbm{k}
-module, together with subsets

\{\alpha_{s}^{\vee}\}_{s\in S}\subseteq\mathfrak{h}\hskip 28.45274pt\{\alpha_{s}\}_{s\in S}\subseteq\mathfrak{h}^{*}

We call
\alpha_{s}
simple roots and
\alpha_{s}^{\vee}
simple coroots of the realization. A realization must satisfy the following conditions:

(1)

\alpha_{s}(\alpha_{s}^{\vee})=2
for all
s\in S
;

(2)

the assignment

S\times\mathfrak{h}\to\mathfrak{h}\hskip 28.45274pt(s,v)\mapsto v-\alpha_{s}(v)\alpha_{s}^{\vee}

extends to a
W
-action on
\mathfrak{h}
;

(3)

for any pair
(s,t)
of distinct simple reflections such that
m_{st}<\infty
, we have

[m_{st}]_{s}=[m_{st}]_{t}=0

where
[m_{st}]_{s},[m_{st}]_{t}
are the 2-colored quantum numbers for
m_{st}
at
x=-\langle\alpha_{s}^{\vee},\alpha_{t}\rangle
and
y=-\langle\alpha_{t}^{\vee},\alpha_{s}\rangle
respectively.

Condition (3) is quite technical and will usually be easily satisfied. See
[
EW16
, §3.1]
for a detailed discussion.

It is useful to construct a category of realizations. Namely, we define a category,
\mathfrak{R}(\mathbbm{k})
whose objects consist of pairs
((W,S),(\mathfrak{h},\{\alpha_{s}\}_{s\in S},\{\alpha_{s}^{\vee}\}_{s\in S}))
where
(\mathfrak{h},\{\alpha_{s}\}_{s\in S},\{\alpha_{s}^{\vee}\}_{s\in S})
is a realization of
(W,S)
over
\mathbbm{k}
.
We will abuse notation and often write
(W,\mathfrak{h})
to denote this pair when no ambiguity is present.
A morphism between realizations
\phi:(W,\mathfrak{h})\to(W^{\prime},\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})
consists of an isomorphism of underlying vector spaces
\phi:\mathfrak{h}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}
along with a
morphism of Coxeter groups
\varphi:W\to W^{\prime}
(i.e.
\varphi
must take simple reflections to either simple reflections or the identity) such that
\phi(w\cdot x)=\varphi(w)\cdot\phi(x)
for
w\in W
and
x\in\mathfrak{h}
.

Definition 4.1.3
.

A realization
(\mathfrak{h},\{\alpha_{s}\}_{s\in S},\{\alpha_{s}^{\vee}\}_{s\in S})
of
(W,S)
is said to be:

(1)

Dezamure surjective
if the maps

\alpha_{s}:\mathfrak{h}\to\mathbbm{k},\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\alpha_{s}^{\vee}:\mathfrak{h}^{*}\to\mathbbm{k}

are surjective for all
s\in S
.

(2)

faithful
if the representation

W\to\GL(\mathfrak{h})\hskip 28.45274ptw\mapsto w\cdot(-)

is faithful.

(3)

reflection faithful
if it is faithful, and for all
w\in W
, the fixed point set
\mathfrak{h}_{w}\subseteq\mathfrak{h}
has codimension one if and only if
w

is a reflection in
W
.

(4)

balanced
if for any pair of distinct simple reflections
(s,t)
, we have

[m_{st}-1]_{s}=[m_{st}-1]_{t}=1.

All of our realizations will be reflection faithful, balanced, and Dezamure surjective. We will write
\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}(\mathbbm{k})
for the full subcategory of
\mathfrak{R}(\mathbbm{k})
consisting of
such realizations.
We will often call realizations in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}(\mathbbm{k})
“nice” to simplify terminology.

Finally, if
\Theta\subset S
, we can associate a parabolic subgroup
W_{\Theta}
of
W
generated by
\Theta
.
For a nice realization
(\mathfrak{h},\{\alpha_{s}\}_{s\in S},\{\alpha_{s}^{\vee}\}_{s\in S})
of
(W,S)
, we can consider the
restricted
realization for
(W_{\Theta},\Theta)
.
The restricted realization consists of the triple
(\mathfrak{h},\{\alpha_{s}\}_{s\in\Theta},\{\alpha_{s}^{\vee}\}_{s\in\Theta})
.

4.1.4.
Basic Definitions

Let
(\mathfrak{h},\{\alpha_{s}\}_{s\in S},\{\alpha_{s}^{\vee}\}_{s\in S})
be a nice realization of
(W,S)
. We will write
R(\mathfrak{h}^{*})
for symmetric algebra on
\mathfrak{h}^{*}

which is graded with
\deg\mathfrak{h}^{*}=2
.
If the realization is fixed or obvious from context, then we will simply write
R
for this symmetric algebra. The contragradient action of
W
on
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
gives rise
to an action of
W
on
R
.

For
w\in W
, define a graded
R
-bimodule
R_{w}
as the quotient of
R\otimes R
generated by the ideal
w(r)\otimes 1-1\otimes r
for
r\in R
.
Alternatively, we can think of
R_{w}
as the
w
-twisting of the standard
R
-bimodule.

For
I\subseteq S
, we define the graded algebra
R^{I}
consisting of the
W_{I}
-invariants of
R
. If
s\in S
, we will write
R^{s}=R^{\{s\}}
.
We will work in the abelian category
R\otimes R\textnormal{-mod}^{\mathbb{Z}}
of graded
R
-bimodules. For each
s\in S
, let
B_{s}
denote the graded
R
-bimodule given by
B_{s}=R\otimes_{R^{s}}R(1)
.
Define the category of
Bott-Samelson bimodules
for
W
, denoted
\textbf{BS}\textnormal{Bim}_{W}(\mathfrak{h})
, as the full monoidal subcategory of
R\otimes R\textnormal{-mod}^{\mathbb{Z}}
generated by tensor
products of the
B_{s}
’s for
s\in S
.
If
\mathbbm{k}
is a field or a complete local ring, we can consider the idempotent completion of the additive hull of
\textbf{BS}\textnormal{Bim}_{W}(\mathfrak{h})
, denoted
\mathbb{S}\textnormal{Bim}_{W}(\mathfrak{h})
, which is
called the category of
Soergel bimodules
.
If the choice of realization is obvious from context, we will write
\textbf{BS}\textnormal{Bim}_{W}
(resp.
\mathbb{S}\textnormal{Bim}_{W}
) for the category of Bott-Samelson bimodules (resp. Soergel bimodules).

4.1.5.
Functorality of Realizations

Assume that
\mathbbm{k}
is a complete local ring or a field.
The category of Soergel bimodules admits a certain degree of functorality over
\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}(\mathbbm{k})
.
Let
(W,\mathfrak{h})
and
(W^{\prime},\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})
be realizations in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}(\mathbbm{k})
. Let
\phi:(W,\mathfrak{h})\to(W^{\prime},\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})
be a morphism of realizations. We can define

{}^{\prime}R_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime},\mathfrak{h}}(\phi)
as the quotient of
R(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}R(\mathfrak{h})
by the ideal generated by
\phi(r)\otimes 1-1\otimes r
for
r\in R(\mathfrak{h})
.
Similarly, we can define

R_{\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}^{\prime}(\phi)
as the quotient of
R(\mathfrak{h})\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}R(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})
by the ideal generated by
r\otimes 1-1\otimes\phi(r)
for
r\in R(\mathfrak{h})
.
Note that
{}^{\prime}R_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime},\mathfrak{h}}(\phi)
(resp.
R_{\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}^{\prime}(\phi)
) is a graded left (resp. right)
R(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})
-module and a graded right (resp. left)

R(\mathfrak{h})
-module, both of rank 1.

We can then consider the functor

\phi_{*}:R(\mathfrak{h})\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}R(\mathfrak{h})\textnormal{-mod}^{\mathbb{Z}}\to R(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}R(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})\textnormal{-mod}^{\mathbb{Z}}

M\mapsto{}^{\prime}R_{\mathfrak{h}^{\prime},\mathfrak{h}}(\phi)\otimes_{R(\mathfrak{h})}M\otimes_{R(\mathfrak{h})}R_{\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}}^{\prime}(\phi).

The equivariance constraint on morphisms of realizations guarantees that
\phi_{*}
restricts to a functor

\phi_{*}:\mathbb{S}\textnormal{Bim}_{W}(\mathfrak{h})\to\mathbb{S}\textnormal{Bim}_{W^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}).

Let
\Cat
denote the 2-category of small categories.
The following lemma follows easily from definitions.

Lemma 4.1.6
.

There is a 2-functor

\mathbb{S}\textnormal{Bim}:\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}(\mathbbm{k})\to\Cat

such that for all morphisms of realizations
\phi:(W,\mathfrak{h})\to(W^{\prime},\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})
,
\mathbb{S}\textnormal{Bim}(\phi)=\phi_{*}
.

In particular, if
\phi
is an isomorphism of realizations, then

\phi_{*}:\mathbb{S}\textnormal{Bim}_{W}(\mathfrak{h})\to\mathbb{S}\textnormal{Bim}_{W^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})

is an equivalence of categories.

4.2.
Finite Parabolics

We will now return to the example of
W
arising from a Kac-Moody group. Let
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbbm{k}}^{*}=\mathbbm{k}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}X(T)
. We have that
R_{\mathbbm{k}}\coloneq R(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbbm{k}}^{*})\cong H_{T}(\pt;\mathbbm{k})
.
When
\mathbbm{k}
is clear from context, we will occasionally write
\mathfrak{h}^{*}\coloneq\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbbm{k}}^{*}
and
R=R_{\mathbbm{k}}
.
Fix a
W
-orbit
\mathfrak{o}
in
\Ch(T,\mathbbm{k})
. Let
\mathcal{L}\in\mathfrak{o}
.

The group
W_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}
is a Coxeter group with simple reflections

S_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}=\{s\in W_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}\mid\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(s)=1\}.

Let
\Theta\subseteq S_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}
be a subset. We denote by
W_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}^{\circ}
the standard parabolic subgroup of
W_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}
generated by
\Theta
.
We say that
\Theta
is of
finite type
if
W_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}^{\circ}
is finite.
If
\Theta
is of finite type, then
W_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}^{\circ}
has a unique maximal element
w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}
.

Let
\beta\in{}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}\underline{W}_{\mathcal{L}}
be a block. For a subset
\Theta\subseteq S_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}
, we can define a subset
\beta(\Theta)=w^{\beta}\Theta w^{\beta,-1}\subseteq S_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}^{\circ}
.
It is clear that if
\Theta
is of finite type, then
\beta(\Theta)
will be of finite type as well.

The isomorphism of
3.1.6
gives rise to an isomorphism of realizations
(W_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ},\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbbm{k}}^{*})\to(W_{w^{\beta}(\mathcal{L})}^{\circ},\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbbm{k}}^{*})
.
The following corollary is an immediate application of Lemma
4.1.6
.

Corollary 4.2.1
.

Let
\beta\in{}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}\underline{W}_{\mathcal{L}}
. Then there is an equivalence of monoidal categories

{}^{\beta}(-):\mathbb{S}\textnormal{Bim}_{W_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}}^{\Theta}(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbbm{k}}^{*})\to\mathbb{S}\textnormal{Bim}_{W_{w^{\beta}(\mathcal{L})}^{\circ}}^{\beta(\Theta)}(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbbm{k}}^{*}).

{}^{\beta}(M)=R_{w^{\beta}}\otimes_{R}M\otimes_{R}R_{w^{\beta,-1}}.

such that for all
w\in W_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}^{\circ}
,

{}^{\beta}(B_{w})\cong B_{w^{\beta}ww^{\beta,-1}}.

Remark 4.2.2
.

In general, finite type parabolic subgroups of
W_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}
do not arise from finite type parabolic subgroups of
G
itself.

4.3.
Endosimple Reflections

A reoccurring problem in working with simple reflections in
W_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}
is that the simple reflections in
W_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}
need not be simple reflections in
W
.
Recall that the simple reflections
W_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}
are called
endosimple reflections
.
Unless explicitly stated, we will only call a reflection
s

simple
if it is simple in
W
. Of course, if
s\in W_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}
is simple, then it is
automatically endosimple.

Let
\beta\in{}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}\underline{W}_{\mathcal{L}}
.
Let
w\in\beta
and let
\underline{w}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{k})
be an expression for
w
in
(W,S)
.
We say that the expression
\underline{w}
is
endo-reduced
if

\ell_{\beta}(w)=\#\{1\leq i\leq k\mid s_{i}\ldots s_{k}\mathcal{L}=s_{i+1}\ldots s_{k}\mathcal{L}\}.

If
\underline{w}
is a reduced, then by Lemma
3.1.9
,
\underline{w}
is endo-reduced. However, the converse need not be true. For example, if
s\in S
satisfies
s\mathcal{L}\neq\mathcal{L}
, then
(s,s)
is not reduced, but it is endo-reduced.

Lemma 4.3.1

(
[
Gou21
, Lemma 5.1.5]
)
.

Let
s\in W_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}
be an endosimple reflection. Let
\underline{s}
be an endo-reduced expression for
s
in
W
. Then

•

\underline{s}
has odd length and is palindromic, i.e.,
\underline{s}=(s_{1},\ldots s_{k},t,s_{k},\ldots,s_{1})
;

•

s_{k}\ldots s_{1}
is minimal in the block
\beta\in{}_{s_{k}\ldots s_{1}\mathcal{L}}\underline{W}_{\mathcal{L}}
containing
s_{k}\ldots s_{1}
;

•

t
is a simple reflection in
W_{w^{\beta}\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}
.

In the non-monodromic study of Hecke categories, an individual simple reflection carries a canonical Frobenius algebra structure.
We wish to extend this to the monodromic case. In particular, there should be a canonical Frobenius algebra structure on
\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}
for each endo-reduced
expression
\underline{s}
of an endosimple reflection
s\in W_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}
.

For each
s\in S_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}
, fix an endo-reduced expression
\underline{s}
of
s
in
W
.
By Lemma
4.3.1
,
\underline{s}
is of the form
\underline{s}=\underline{x}t\overline{\underline{x}}
.
We will write
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}=\overline{\underline{x}}\mathcal{L}
. By assumption,
t
is a simple reflection in
W_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}^{\circ}
.

4.3.2.
Unit and Counit

The counit of adjunction produces a map

\epsilon^{\prime}:\mathcal{E}_{t}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}\to j_{e*}j_{e}^{*}\mathcal{E}_{t}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}\cong\mathcal{E}_{e}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}[1].

We can then define the counit map for
\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}
, denoted
\epsilon_{s}
, as the composition

(4.3.1)

\epsilon_{s}:\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{E}_{\underline{x}}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}\star\epsilon^{\prime}\star\mathcal{E}_{\overline{\underline{x}}}^{\mathcal{L}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{E}_{\underline{x}}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}\star\mathcal{E}_{e}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}\star\mathcal{E}_{\overline{\underline{x}}}^{\mathcal{L}}[1]\cong\mathcal{E}_{e}^{\mathcal{L}}[1].

Alternatively, it can be checked that
\epsilon_{s}
is itself given by the unit of the adjunction
\id\to j_{e*}j_{e}^{*}
.

A symmetric argument gives rise to the unit map for
\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}
,

(4.3.2)

\eta_{s}:\mathcal{E}_{e}^{\mathcal{L}}\to\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}[1],

coming from the adjunction morphism

\eta^{\prime}:\mathcal{E}_{e}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}[-1]\cong j_{e*}j_{e}^{!}\mathcal{E}_{t}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{E}_{t}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}.

Alternatively,
\eta_{s}
is given by the counit of the adjunction
j_{e*}j_{e}^{!}\to\id
.

4.3.3.
Multiplication and Comultiplication

Lemma 4.3.4
.

Let
s\in S
be a simple reflection in
W_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}
. Then there is an isomorphism

\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}[-1]\oplus\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}[1].

Proof.

Consider the distinguished triangle

\Delta_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\to\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\to\Delta_{e}^{\mathcal{L}}[1]\to.

We can apply
(-)\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}
to obtain a distinguished triangle

(4.3.3)

\Delta_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\to\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\to\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}[1]\to.

By Lemmas
3.2.9
,
3.2.8
, and
3.3.5
, we have an isomorphism
\Delta_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}[-1]
.
The connecting morphism
\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}[1]\to\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}
in (
4.3.3
) must be zero since
\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}
is perverse.
Therefore, the distinguished triangle (
4.3.3
) splits.
∎

Lemma 4.3.5
.

Let
\underline{s}
be as above.
There exists an isomorphism

\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}[-1]\oplus\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}[1].

Proof.

The isomorphism follows immediately from Lemma
4.3.4
and Proposition
3.3.17
.
∎

Lemma 4.3.6
.

(1)

The morphism

b_{1}:\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}[1]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\id\star\epsilon_{s}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\mathcal{E}_{e}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}

can be identified under an isomorphism from Lemma
4.3.5
with the projection

\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}\oplus\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}[2]\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}.

Moreover, composition with
b_{1}
induces an isomorphism

\tilde{b}_{1}:\Hom(\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}[-1])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Hom(\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}).

(2)

The morphism

b_{2}:\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}[-1]\cong\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\mathcal{E}_{e}^{\mathcal{L}}[-1]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\id\star\eta_{s}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}

can be identified under an isomorphism from Lemma
4.3.5
with the inclusion

\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}[-1]\hookrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}[1]\oplus\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}[-1].

Moreover, pre-composition with
b_{2}
induces an isomorphism

\tilde{b}_{2}:\Hom(\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}[-1])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Hom(\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}[-1],\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}[-1]).

Proof.

We will only prove (1) as the proof of (2) can be obtained by a similar argument.
Note that there is an isomorphism,
\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}
.
Since
\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}
are perverse, there are no negative self-extensions, so we have an isomorphism

\Hom(\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}\oplus\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}[2],\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}})\cong\Hom(\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}).

By Corollary
3.3.9
and Lemma
4.3.5
,

\displaystyle\Hom(\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}})

\displaystyle\cong\Hom(\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{E}_{e}^{\mathcal{L}})

\displaystyle\cong\Hom(\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}[-1]\oplus\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}[1],\mathcal{E}_{e}^{\mathcal{L}})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbbm{k}.

Since
b_{1}
corresponds to an invertible element of
\Hom(\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}})\cong\mathbbm{k}
, by potentially scaling the isomorphism from Lemma

4.3.5
, we can ensure that
b_{1}
indeed identifies with the projection map.
Under this identification, it easily follows that
\tilde{b}_{1}
is an isomorphism.
∎

Following Lemma
4.3.6
, we can define morphisms

\nu_{s}=\tilde{b}_{1}^{-1}(\id):\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}\to\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}[-1]\hskip 28.45274pt\mu_{s}=\tilde{b}_{2}^{-1}(\id):\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}\to\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}[-1]

The following lemma is easy to check from definitions.

Lemma 4.3.7
.

The maps
(\mu_{s},\nu_{s},\epsilon_{s},\eta_{s})
make
\mathcal{E}_{\underline{s}}^{\mathcal{L}}
into a graded Frobenius algebra of degree 1.

4.3.8.
Parabolic Monodromic Subcategories

Let
\Theta\subseteq S_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}
.
We define a full triangulated subcategory
{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\mathcal{D}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ,\Theta}(\mathbbm{k})
of
{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\mathcal{D}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}(\mathbbm{k})
generated by
\Delta_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}
(or

\nabla_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}
) for
w\in W_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}^{\circ}
.
By Proposition
3.3.10
,
{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\mathcal{D}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ,\Theta}(\mathbbm{k})
is closed under convolution.

Suppose that
\mathbbm{k}
is a field or a complete local ring.
We define a full subcategory
{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\textnormal{Par}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ,\Theta}(\mathbbm{k})
of
{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\textnormal{Par}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}(\mathbbm{k})
generated by
\mathcal{E}_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}
for
w\in W_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}^{\circ}
.
It is clear from the construction of
{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\textnormal{Par}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ,\Theta}(\mathbbm{k})
that

{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\textnormal{Par}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ,\Theta}(\mathbbm{k})={}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\textnormal{Par}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}(\mathbbm{k})\cap{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\mathcal{D}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ,\Theta}(\mathbbm{k}).

By Theorem
3.4.2
,
{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\textnormal{Par}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ,\Theta}(\mathbbm{k})
is closed under convolution.

We call these subcategories
parabolic monodromic subcategories
. We are particularly interested when
\Theta
is of finite type.
In such a situation,
{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\mathcal{D}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ,\Theta}(\mathbbm{k})
contains only finitely many isomorphism classes of simple perverse sheaves.

For each
\beta\in{}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}\underline{W}_{\mathcal{L}}
. Define a functor

{}^{\beta}(-):{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\mathcal{D}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}(\mathbbm{k})\to{}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\backslash}\mathcal{D}_{/\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}^{\circ}(\mathbbm{k})

\mathcal{F}\mapsto\IC_{w^{\beta}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\mathcal{F}\star\IC_{w^{\beta,-1}}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}.

This functor restricts to a functor between parabolic monodromic subcategories

{}^{\beta}(-):{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\textnormal{Par}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ,\Theta}(\mathbbm{k})\to{}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\backslash}\textnormal{Par}_{/\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}^{\circ,\beta(\Theta)}(\mathbbm{k}).

4.4.
Maximal IC Sheaves

The main benefit in working in the parabolic monodromic subcategories is they allow us to construct maximal IC sheaves. In this section, we will study these sheaves
along with rigidified variants.
In order for maximal IC sheaves to exist, we will always assume that
\Theta\subseteq S_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}
is of finite type.

We will also start to make the assumption that
\mathbbm{k}
is a field of characteristic 0 which will continue until §
5
.
It can easily be checked that with characteristic 0 coefficients the category of parity sheaves coincides with the category of semisimple complexes.

Let
\beta\in{}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}\underline{W}_{\mathcal{L}}
.
We define the
\Theta
-maximal IC sheaf as
\IC_{w_{\beta,\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}
. When
\Theta=S
is of finite type, then the
\Theta
-maximal IC sheaf recovers the usual
notion of maximal IC sheaf of
[
LY20
]
.

When
\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}=\underline{\mathbbm{k}}_{T}
,
\Theta
corresponds to an actual parabolic subgroup
P_{\Theta}
of
G
.
In this case, the
\Theta
-maximal IC sheaf corresponds to the shifted constant sheaf
\underline{\mathbbm{k}}_{B\backslash P_{\Theta}/B}[\dim P_{\Theta}/B]
which is

!
-extended to all of
B\backslash G/B
.
The constant sheaf
\underline{\mathbbm{k}}_{B\backslash P_{\Theta}/B}
has two crucial properties in defining a restricted parabolic variant of Soergel theory.

(1)

The stalks and costalks at
\dot{w}
of
\underline{\mathbbm{k}}_{B\backslash P_{\Theta}/B}
are one-dimensional when
w\in W_{\Theta}
and 0 when
w\notin W_{\Theta}
.

(2)

Any convolution
\underline{\mathbbm{k}}_{B\backslash P_{\Theta}/B}\star\IC_{w}^{\underline{\mathbbm{k}}}
for
w\in W_{\Theta}
yields direct sums of shifts of
\underline{\mathbbm{k}}_{B\backslash P_{\Theta}/B}
.

These properties allow one to define a (canonical) coalgebra structure on
\underline{\mathbbm{k}}_{B\backslash P_{\Theta}/B}
. As a result, the Soergel functor

\Hom^{\bullet}(\underline{\mathbbm{k}}_{B\backslash P_{\Theta}/B},-):{}_{\underline{\mathbbm{k}}\backslash}\textnormal{Par}_{/\underline{\mathbbm{k}}}^{\Theta}(\mathbbm{k})\to\left(\End^{\bullet}(\IC_{e}^{\underline{\mathbbm{k}}})\otimes\End^{\bullet}(\IC_{e}^{\underline{\mathbbm{k}}})\right)\textnormal{-mod}^{\mathbb{Z}}

is lax-monoidal. The goal of this section is to develop versions of these facts for
\Theta
-maximal IC sheaves. As a result, we can develop a monodromic version of the
Soergel functor.
We note that when
W
is finite and
\Theta=S
, there are similar results covered in
[
LY20
, §6]
.
However, we need to modify some of their arguments to make the parabolic variants work.

4.4.1.
Convolution with Maximal IC Sheaves

Lemma 4.4.2
.

Let
s\in W_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}
be a simple reflection. Let
\beta\in{}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}\underline{W}_{\mathcal{L}}
and
w\in\beta
.

(1)

If
w<ws
, then there is an isomorphism

(4.4.1)

\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\IC_{ws}^{\mathcal{L}}\oplus\mathcal{P}_{<_{\beta}ws}

where
\mathcal{P}_{<_{\beta}ws}
is a semisimple perverse sheaf of the form

\mathcal{P}_{<_{\beta}ws}=\bigoplus_{x<_{\beta}ws}\IC_{x}^{\mathcal{L}}\otimes V_{x}

and
V_{x}
is a
\mathbbm{k}
-vector space. Moreover,
V_{x}=0
if
\ell(x)=\ell(ws)(\textnormal{mod }2)
.

(2)

If
w>ws
, then there is an isomorphism

(4.4.2)

\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}[-1]\oplus\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}[1].

Proof.

(1):
Assume
w<ws
. We will first show that
\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}
is perverse. To simplify notation, we will write
\left({}^{p}D^{\leq 0},{}^{p}D^{\geq 0}\right)
for the perverse
t
-structure on
{}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\backslash}\mathcal{D}_{/\mathcal{L}}(\mathbbm{k})
.
In particular, we will show that
\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\in{}^{p}D^{\leq 0}
. The case of
\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\in{}^{p}D^{\geq 0}
follows
from Verdier duality.
By Theorem
3.8.4
, it follows from a standard combinatorial argument that

(4.4.3)

\langle\underline{H}_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}\underline{H}_{s}^{\mathcal{L}},T_{x}^{\mathcal{L}}\rangle=\begin{cases}v^{-\ell(w)-1}\left(P_{x,w}^{\mathcal{L}}+v^{2}P_{xs,w}^{\mathcal{L}}\right)&\text{if }x<xs,\\
v^{-\ell(w)-1}\left(P_{xs,w}^{\mathcal{L}}+v^{2}P_{x,w}^{\mathcal{L}}\right)&\text{if }x>xs.\end{cases}

In particular
\langle\underline{H}_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}\underline{H}_{s}^{\mathcal{L}},T_{x}^{\mathcal{L}}\rangle
has degree
\leq-\ell(x)
for all
x\in\beta
.
As a consequence,
\dim H^{i}(j_{x}^{*}(\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}))=0
when
i>-\ell(x)
. Therefore,
\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\in{}^{p}D^{\leq 0}
.

Moreover, (
4.4.3
) implies that the restrictions of
\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}
along
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}\ell}_{x}
is only nonzero if
x\in\{w,ws\}
or
x\leq_{\beta}w
.
In particular, the stalks can only be nonzero when
x\leq_{\beta}ws
.
This stalk computation along with Theorem
3.4.2
implies that
\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}
admits a decomposition,

\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\IC_{ws}^{\mathcal{L}}\oplus\mathcal{P}_{<_{\beta}ws}

where
\mathcal{P}_{<_{\beta}ws}
is a semisimple perverse sheaf of the form

\mathcal{P}_{<_{\beta}ws}=\bigoplus_{x<_{\beta}ws}\IC_{x}^{\mathcal{L}}\otimes V_{x}.

if
\ell(w)
is even (resp.
\ell(w)
is odd), then
\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}
is even (resp. odd).
As a result, the proof of Lemma
3.4.4
ensures that
\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}
has the opposite parity of
\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}
.
Therefore,
V_{x}=0
if
\ell(x)=\ell(ws)(\textnormal{mod }2)
.

(2):
Assume
w>ws
. By part (1), there is an isomorphism

(4.4.4)

\IC_{ws}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\oplus\mathcal{P}_{<_{\beta}w}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}.

On the other hand, part (1) along with Lemma
4.3.4
yields an isomorphism

(4.4.5)

\IC_{ws}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}[-1]\oplus\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}[1]\oplus\mathcal{P}_{<_{\beta}w}[-1]\oplus\mathcal{P}_{<_{\beta}w}[-1].

The parity conditions on
\mathcal{P}_{<_{\beta}w}
ensures that if
\IC_{x}^{\mathcal{L}}
is a summand of
\mathcal{P}_{<_{\beta}w}
, we have that
j_{w}^{*}(\IC_{x}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}})=0
.
Therefore, by comparing (
4.4.4
) with (
4.4.5
), we obtain an isomorphism

\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}[-1]\oplus\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}[1].

∎

Proposition 4.4.3
.

Let
w\in W_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}^{\circ}
.

(1)

The convolution
\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}
is isomorphic to a direct sum of shifts of
\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}
.

(2)

The perverse cohomology
{}^{p}H^{i}(\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}})=0
unless
-\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w)\leq i\leq\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w)
and
i\equiv\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w)\textnormal{ }(\textnormal{mod }2)
.

(3)

There is an isomorphism

{}^{p}H^{\pm\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w)}(\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}})\cong\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}.

Proof.

We will argue by induction on
\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w)
.
We can first consider the base cases of
\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w)=0
and
\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w)=1
. When
\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w)=0
, we have that
w=e
, and so (1), (2), (3) follow

\IC_{e}^{\mathcal{L}}
being the monoidal unit.
Now suppose that
\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w)=1
, i.e.,
w=s\in\Theta
an endosimple reflection.
By Lemma
4.3.1
, we can find some block
\beta\in{}_{\mathcal{L}}\underline{W}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}
such that
s=w^{\beta}tw^{\beta,-1}
for
t
a simple reflection in

W_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}^{\circ}
.
By Proposition
3.3.17
there is an isomorphism

\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}w^{\beta}}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}\star\IC_{t}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}\star\IC_{w^{\beta,-1}}^{\mathcal{L}}

By maximality in
W_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}^{\circ}
,
\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}s)<\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta})
. As a result, by
(
3.1.1
), we have that
\ell(w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}w^{\beta}t)<\ell(w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}w^{\beta})
.
By Lemma
4.4.2
(2), we obtain an isomorphism

\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}w^{\beta}}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}\star\IC_{t}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}\cong\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}w^{\beta}}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}[-1]\oplus\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}w^{\beta}}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}[1].

We can convolve the above isomorphisms with
\IC_{w^{\beta,-1}}^{\mathcal{L}}
to obtain

(4.4.6)

\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\IC_{w_{\beta,\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}[-1]\oplus\IC_{w_{\beta,\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}[1].

Let
\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w)>1
. We can then find some
s\in\Theta
such that
w^{\prime}=ws
with
\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w^{\prime})<\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w)
.
By the inductive hypothesis,

(4.4.7)

\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{w^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}\otimes V^{\prime}

where
V^{\prime}=\bigoplus_{n=-\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w^{\prime})}^{\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w^{\prime})}V_{n}^{\prime}[-n]
where
\dim V_{n}^{\prime}=1
if
n=\pm\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w^{\prime})
.
From (
4.4.6
) and (
4.4.7
), we obtain an isomorphism

(4.4.8)

\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{w^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}\otimes V^{\prime\prime}

where
V^{\prime\prime}=V^{\prime}[-1]\oplus V^{\prime}[1]
. By Lemma
4.4.2
and Proposition
3.3.17
, we have an isomorphism
\IC_{w^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}\oplus\mathcal{P}_{<_{\mathcal{L}}w}
where
\mathcal{P}_{<_{\mathcal{L}}w}
is a semisimple perverse sheaf supported on
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}\ell}_{<w}
.
As a result,

\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}\oplus\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\mathcal{P}_{<_{\mathcal{L}}w}\cong\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}\otimes V^{\prime\prime}.

It is then clear that (1) and (2) hold. For
x\in W_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}^{\circ}
with
\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(x)<\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w)
, the inductive hypothesis yields an isomorphism

\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{x}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}\otimes V_{x}

where
V_{x}=\bigoplus_{n=-\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(x)}^{\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(x)}V_{x,n}[-n]
. Since all
\IC_{x}^{\mathcal{L}}
-summands of
\mathcal{P}_{<_{\mathcal{L}}w}
satisfy that
\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(x)<\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w)
we have that

(4.4.9)

\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\mathcal{P}_{<_{\mathcal{L}}w}\cong\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}\otimes V^{\prime\prime\prime}

where
V^{\prime\prime\prime}
is a formal chain complex of
\mathbbm{k}
-vector spaces concentrated in degrees
n
where
-\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w)<n<\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w)
.
By comparing the
V^{\prime\prime}
from (
4.4.8
) and the
V^{\prime\prime\prime}
from (
4.4.9
), we see that

{}^{p}H^{n}(\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}})\cong\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}

when
n=\pm\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w)
which completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

4.4.4.
Stalks of Maximal IC Sheaves

Proposition 4.4.5
.

For
w\in W_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}^{\circ}
, we have isomorphisms

(1)

j_{w}^{*}\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\mathcal{K}_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}[\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta})+\ell(w)-\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w)],

(2)

j_{w}^{!}\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\mathcal{K}_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}[-\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta})+\ell(w)+\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w)].

Proof.

It suffices to just prove the first isomorphism as the second will follow from Verdier duality.

Since
\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}
is a parity sheaf, we have that
j_{w}^{*}\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong V_{w}\otimes_{H_{T}^{\bullet}(\pt;\mathbbm{k})}\mathcal{K}_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}
where
V_{w}=\bigoplus_{n}V_{w}^{n}[n]
is a graded
H_{T}^{\bullet}(\pt;\mathbbm{k})
-module
8
8

8

Here we are using the identification given in Proposition

3.2.2
to view sheaves on
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}\ell}_{w}
as equivariant sheaves on a point.
which can be computed by

V_{w}^{n}=\Hom(j_{w}^{*}\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{K}_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}[-n]).

The proposition can then be reformulated as

(4.4.10)

\Hom^{\bullet}(\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}},\nabla_{w}^{\mathcal{L}})\cong H^{\bullet}_{T}(\pt;\mathbbm{k})[-\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta})+\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w)]

We will argue by backwards induction on
\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w)
. If
\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w)
is maximal, i.e.,
w=w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}
, then the isomorphism is obvious.
If
\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w)
is not maximal, then we can find some
s\in\Theta
such that
w<_{\mathcal{L}}ws
.
By Lemma
4.3.1
, we can find some block
\beta\in{}_{\mathcal{L}}\underline{W}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}
such that
s=w^{\beta}tw^{\beta,-1}
for
t
a simple reflection in

W_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}^{\circ}
.
By Proposition
3.3.17
, we have an isomorphism

\Hom^{\bullet}(\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}},\nabla_{w}^{\mathcal{L}})\cong\Hom^{\bullet}(\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}w^{\beta}}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}},\nabla_{ww^{\beta}}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}})

Since
\ell_{\beta}(w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}w^{\beta}t)<\ell_{\beta}(w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}w^{\beta})
, we can apply Lemma
3.2.8
to show that the stalk of

\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}w^{\beta}}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}
at lifts of
ww^{\beta}
and
ww^{\beta}t
are isomorphic up to a shift.
This induces an isomorphism of Hom-spaces,

\Hom^{\bullet}(\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}w^{\beta}}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}},\nabla_{ww^{\beta}}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}})\cong\Hom^{\bullet}(\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}w^{\beta}}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}},\nabla_{ww^{\beta}t}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}[-1]).

Finally, by another application of Proposition
3.3.17
and the inductive hypothesis, we get isomorphisms

\displaystyle\Hom^{\bullet}(\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}},\nabla_{w}^{\mathcal{L}})

\displaystyle\cong\Hom^{\bullet}(\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}w^{\beta}}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}},\nabla_{ww^{\beta}t}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}[-1])

\displaystyle\cong\Hom^{\bullet}(\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}},\nabla_{ws}^{\mathcal{L}}[-1])

\displaystyle\cong H_{T}^{\bullet}(\pt;\mathbbm{k})[-\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta})+\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(ws)-1]

\displaystyle\cong H_{T}^{\bullet}(\pt;\mathbbm{k})[-\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta})+\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w)].

This completes the proof by the characterization of the proposition given in (
4.4.10
).
∎

Notation 4.4.6
.

It is useful to consider a shifted version of the maximal IC sheaves to act as the replacement for constant sheaves.
We will write
\mathcal{K}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\Theta}=\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}[-\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta})]
.

Remark 4.4.7
.

There are extensions of Proposition
4.4.3
and Proposition
4.4.5
to sheaves outside the neutral block.
Such generalizations necessitate a more general definition of the parabolic monodromic subcategories. For example, if
\beta\in{}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}\underline{W}_{\mathcal{L}}
, one could
consider the IC-sheaf
\IC_{w^{\beta}w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}}
which behaves like a maximal IC sheaf for the block
\beta
.
We do not need these generalizations for this paper, but similar results in this direction when
W
is finite can be found in
[
LY20
, §6]
.

4.4.8.
Coalgebra Structure on Maximal IC Sheaves

Let
\Theta\subset S_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}
be of finite type.
By Proposition
4.4.5
and adjunction, we have isomorphisms

\Hom(\mathcal{K}^{\mathcal{L}}_{\Theta},\IC_{e}^{\mathcal{L}})\cong\Hom(\mathcal{K}_{e}^{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{K}_{e}^{\mathcal{L}}).

As a result, there is a morphism
\epsilon_{\mathcal{L}}:\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}\to\IC_{e}^{\mathcal{L}}
corresponding to the identity map
\id:\mathcal{K}_{e}^{\mathcal{L}}\to\mathcal{K}_{e}^{\mathcal{L}}
.

Proposition 4.4.9
.

There is a unique coalgebra structure on
\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}
with counit map
\epsilon_{\mathcal{L}}
.

Before proving Proposition
4.4.9
, we must introduce some notation for handling iterated convolutions of minimal and maximal ICs. For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, we write

(\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}})^{\star n}\coloneq\underbrace{\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\ldots\star\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}}_{n\textnormal{-fold}}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt(\IC_{e}^{\mathcal{L}})^{\star n}\coloneq\underbrace{\IC_{e}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\ldots\star\IC_{e}^{\mathcal{L}}}_{n\textnormal{-fold}}.

By convention, we set
(\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}})^{\star 0}=\IC_{e}^{\mathcal{L}}=(\IC_{e}^{\mathcal{L}})^{\star 0}
.
Similarly, we can define a morphism
\epsilon_{\mathcal{L}}^{n}:(\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}})^{\star n}\to(\IC_{e}^{\mathcal{L}})^{\star n}
as the
n
-fold convolution of
\epsilon_{\mathcal{L}}
.
Of course, there are canonical isomorphisms
(\IC_{e}^{\mathcal{L}})^{\star n}\cong\IC_{e}^{\mathcal{L}}
.

Lemma 4.4.10
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, there is a unique morphism

\mu_{\mathcal{L}}^{n}:\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}\to(\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}})^{\star n}

making the following diagram commute

(4.4.11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}})^{\star n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\IC_{e}^{\mathcal{L}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\IC_{e}^{\mathcal{L}})^{\star n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\mathcal{L}}^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{\mathcal{L}}^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Proof.

We can iterate Proposition
4.4.3
(3) to obtain an isomorphism

(\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}})^{\star n}\cong\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}\otimes V

where

V=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{2n\cdot\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta})}V_{-i}[i]

and
\dim V_{0}=1
. As a result, we can see that
\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}
occurs with multiplicity 1 in
(\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}})^{\star n}
.
Moreover, since
V_{i}=0
whenever
i>0
, it follows that

\dim\Hom(\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}},(\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}})^{\star n})=1.

Let
\mu^{n}_{\mathcal{L}}:\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}\to(\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}})^{\star n}
be any nonzero morphism.

We claim that the composition

(4.4.12)

\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{\mathcal{L}}^{n}}}{{\to}}(\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}})^{\star n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\epsilon_{\mathcal{L}}^{n}}}{{\to}}(\IC_{e}^{\mathcal{L}})^{\star n}\cong\IC_{e}^{\mathcal{L}}.

is nonzero. We will prove this claim for
n=2
as the cases of
n>2
can be obtained by a routine induction argument (c.f.,
[
LY20
, Proposition 6.6]
).
Since
\epsilon_{\mathcal{L}}^{2}=(\epsilon_{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{e}^{\mathcal{L}})\circ(\IC_{e}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\epsilon_{\mathcal{L}})
and from Proposition

4.4.3
, we obtain that

{}^{p}H^{0}(\epsilon_{\mathcal{L}}^{2})
is surjective. As we remarked before,
{}^{p}H^{0}((\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}})^{\star 2})\cong\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}
. hence,
{}^{p}H^{0}(\mu_{\mathcal{L}}^{2})
is an isomorphism.
In particular,
{}^{p}H^{0}(\mu_{\mathcal{L}}^{2}\circ\epsilon_{\mathcal{L}}^{2})
is nonzero which proves the claim.

Since
\Hom(\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}},\IC_{e}^{\mathcal{L}})
is one-dimensional and since (
4.4.12
) is nonzero,
\mu_{\mathcal{L}}^{n}
is uniquely determined
by some scalar which makes diagram (
4.4.11
) commute.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.4.9
.

In light of Lemma
4.4.10
, we only need to show that
\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}
is a coalgebra with comultiplication
\mu_{\mathcal{L}}^{2}
and counit

\epsilon_{\mathcal{L}}
.
The argument is essentially given in
[
LY20
, Proposition 6.6]
.
The coassociativity axioms follows from the uniqueness of
\mu_{\mathcal{L}}^{3}
.
The counit axioms follow from the commutativity of (
4.4.11
).

\square

4.5.
Soergel
\mathbb{H}
-functor

4.5.1.
Definition

Let
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\in{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\mathcal{D}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}(\mathbbm{k})
. We have an isomorphism

\Hom^{\bullet}_{{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\mathcal{D}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})\cong H_{T\times T}^{\bullet}(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}\ell},\textbf{R}\mathcal{H}om(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})).

As a result, there is an action of
R_{\mathbbm{k}}\coloneq H_{T}^{\bullet}(\pt;\mathbbm{k})
on both the left and right of
\Hom^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
which makes
\Hom^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
a graded
R_{\mathbbm{k}}
-bimodule.

For a subset
\Theta\subset S_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}
of finite type, we define the Soergel
\mathbb{H}
-functor,

\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}:{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\mathcal{D}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ,\Theta}(\mathbbm{k})\to R_{\mathbbm{k}}\otimes R_{\mathbbm{k}}\textnormal{-mod}^{\mathbb{Z}},\hskip 28.45274pt\mathcal{F}\mapsto\Hom^{\bullet}(\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{F}).

4.5.2.
Behavior on Costandards

We first will describe the behavior of the
\mathbb{H}
-functor on costandard objects.

Lemma 4.5.3
.

For any
w\in W_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}^{\circ}
, there is a canonical isomorphism of graded
R
-bimodules,

\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(j_{w*}j_{w}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}})\cong R_{w}.

Moreover, in the case of
w=e
, we have that the map
\epsilon_{\mathcal{L}}:\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}\to\IC_{e}^{\mathcal{L}}
corresponds to
1\in R
.

Proof.

By adjunction and Proposition
4.4.5
, we obtain canonical isomorphisms of graded
R
-bimodules,

\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\nabla_{w}^{\mathcal{L}})\cong\Hom^{\bullet}(\mathcal{K}_{w}^{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{K}_{w}^{\mathcal{L}})\cong H_{\Gamma(w)}^{\bullet}(\pt;\mathbbm{k}).

It is easy to check that the action of
R=H_{T}^{\bullet}(\pt;\mathbbm{k})
on
H_{\Gamma(w)}^{\bullet}(\pt;\mathbbm{k})
gives an isomorphism
H_{\Gamma(w)}^{\bullet}(\pt;\mathbbm{k})\cong R_{w}
.

When
w=e
, the definition of
\epsilon_{\mathcal{L}}
ensures that it identifies with
1\in R
.
∎

4.5.4.
Monoidal Structure

In this section, we will show that the
\mathbb{H}
-functor is monoidal after restricting to the category of parity sheaves.

By Proposition
4.4.9
,
\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}
is lax-monoidal. It will be useful to unpack what this entails.
Let
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\in{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\mathcal{D}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ,\Theta}(\mathbbm{k})
.
For all
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
, we can define the map

\displaystyle\Hom(\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{G}[i])\times\Hom(\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{F}[j])

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{\to}}\Hom(\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{G}\star\mathcal{F}[i+j])

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(-)\circ\mu_{\mathcal{L}}^{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Hom(\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{G}\star\mathcal{F}[i+j]).

By taking direct sums over all
i,j
, we get an induced pairing

\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G})\times\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F})\to\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G}\star\mathcal{F}).

The pairing is
R
-balanced and hence induces a morphism of graded
R
-bimodules

(4.5.1)

c_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F}):\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G})\otimes_{R}\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F})\to\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G}\star\mathcal{F}).

Note that
c_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F})
is functorial in
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}
, so we obtain a natural transformation of bifunctors

c_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}:\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(-)\otimes_{R}\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(-)\implies\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(-\star-).

Since
\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}
is only lax-monoidal, the morphisms
c_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F})
are generally not isomorphisms.
Our next goal will be to build up increasingly more general classes of objects
in which
c_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F})
is an isomorphism.

Lemma 4.5.5
.

Let
\beta\in{}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}\underline{W}_{\mathcal{L}}
be a block.

(1)

The following diagram commutes up to a canonical isomorphism

(4.5.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\mathcal{D}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ,\Theta}(\mathbbm{k})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\mathbbm{k}}\otimes R_{\mathbbm{k}}\textnormal{-mod}^{\mathbb{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\backslash}\mathcal{D}_{/\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}^{\circ,\beta(\Theta)}(\mathbbm{k})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\mathbbm{k}}\otimes R_{\mathbbm{k}}\textnormal{-mod}^{\mathbb{Z}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{\beta}(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{\beta}(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{H}_{\beta(\Theta)}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}}

Moreover, these isomorphisms are compatible for composable blocks
\beta,\gamma
.

(2)

For
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\in{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\mathcal{D}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ,\Theta}(\mathbbm{k})
,
{}^{\beta}(c_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F}))
identifies with
c_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}({}^{\beta}\mathcal{G},{}^{\beta}\mathcal{F})
via the commutativity of (
4.5.2
)

Proof.

Note that there is a unique isomorphism
\varphi:\mathcal{K}_{\beta(\Theta)}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}\to\IC_{w^{\beta}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{w^{\beta,-1}}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}
which makes the following diagram commute

(4.5.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}_{\beta(\Theta)}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\IC_{w^{\beta}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{w^{\beta,-1}}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\IC_{e}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\IC_{w^{\beta}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{e}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{w^{\beta,-1}}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id\star\epsilon_{\mathcal{L}}\star\id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

After unpacking definitions, we see that
\varphi
is enough to prove (1).

By Proposition
4.4.9
and the construction of
\varphi:\mathcal{K}_{\beta(\Theta)}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}\to\IC_{w^{\beta}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{w^{\beta,-1}}^{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}
in (1), we have that
\varphi
is an isomorphism of coalgebras.
This is enough to prove (2).
∎

Lemma 4.5.6
.

Let
s\in W_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}^{\circ}
be an endosimple reflection.

(1)

Let
\mathcal{F}\in{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\mathcal{D}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ,\Theta}(\mathbbm{k})
. There is an isomorphism of graded
R_{\mathbbm{k}}
-bimodules

\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F})\otimes_{R^{s}}R(1)\cong\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}).

(2)

There is an isomorphism of graded
R_{\mathbbm{k}}
-bimodules

\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}})\cong B_{s}.

(3)

The canonical map
\xi_{s}:\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}\to\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}[-1]
corresponding to the counit map
\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}[-1]\oplus\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}[1]\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}[-1]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\epsilon_{\mathcal{L}}}}{{\to}}\IC_{e}^{\mathcal{L}}

under biadjunction of
\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}
identifies with
1\otimes 1
in
B_{s}[-1]
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.3.1
and Lemma
4.5.5
, it suffices to consider
s\in W_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}^{\circ}
a simple reflection.
Let
\mathcal{F}\in{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\mathcal{D}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ,\Theta}(\mathbbm{k})
. By assumption,
\mathcal{F}
is supported on
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}\ell}_{\leq w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}
. We will write
X=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}\ell}_{\leq w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}/T
and
X^{s}=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}\ell}_{\leq w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}/L_{s}
.

(1):

Extend
\mathcal{L}
to some
\mathcal{L}^{s}\in\Ch(L_{s},\mathbbm{k})
via Lemma
3.2.4
.
By Lemma
3.2.8
, we get an isomorphism,

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\cong\Hom^{\bullet}(\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}^{s}}[-\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta})+1],\pi_{s*}\mathcal{F})

(4.5.4)

\displaystyle\cong H^{\bullet}_{T}(X^{s},\textbf{R}\mathcal{H}om(\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}^{s}}[-\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta})+1],\pi_{s*}\mathcal{F})).

In particular,
\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F})
naturally has the structure of a graded
(R,R^{s})
-bimodule via the identification
R^{s}\cong H^{\bullet}_{L_{s}}(\pt;\mathbbm{k})
.

For
\mathcal{G}\in D(T\backslash X^{s},\mathbbm{k})
, the pullback along the morphism
\pi_{s}:X\to X^{s}
induces an isomorphism of graded
R
-bimodules,

H^{\bullet}_{T}(X,\pi_{s}^{*}\mathcal{G})\cong H^{\bullet}_{T}(X^{s},\mathcal{G}\otimes\pi_{s*}\underline{\mathbbm{k}})\cong H^{\bullet}_{T}(X^{s},\mathcal{G})\otimes_{R^{s}}R.

We can apply the above isomorphism to (
4.5.4
) to obtain isomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F})\otimes_{R^{s}}R(1)

\displaystyle\cong H^{\bullet}_{T}(X^{s},\pi_{s}^{*}\textbf{R}\mathcal{H}om(\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}^{s}}[-\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta})+1],\pi_{s*}\mathcal{F}[1]))

\displaystyle\cong H^{\bullet}_{T}(X,\textbf{R}\mathcal{H}om(\pi_{s}^{*}\IC_{w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}}^{\mathcal{L}^{s}}[-\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w_{\mathcal{L},\Theta})+1],\pi_{s}^{*}\pi_{s*}\mathcal{F}[1]))

\displaystyle\cong H^{\bullet}_{T}(X,\textbf{R}\mathcal{H}om(\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{F}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}))

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}).

Note we are using here the push-pull lemma (Lemma
3.3.5
).

(2):
The statement easily follows from (1) and Lemma
4.5.3
by taking
\mathcal{F}=\IC_{e}^{\mathcal{L}}
.

(3):
Note that
\xi_{s}
is the unique morphism
\mathcal{K}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}\to\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}[-1]
which satisfies
\epsilon_{s}\circ\xi_{s}=\epsilon_{\mathcal{L}}
.
Then
\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\xi_{s})
is the unique element of
R\otimes_{R^{s}}R
such that
m_{s}(\xi_{s})=1
where
m:R\otimes_{R^{s}}R\to R
is the multiplication map.
As a result,
\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\xi_{s})=1\otimes 1
.
∎

Lemma 4.5.7
.

Let
s\in W_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}^{\circ}
be an endosimple reflection. Let
\mathcal{F}\in{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\mathcal{D}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ,\Theta}(\mathbbm{k})
.
Then the map
c_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F},\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}):\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F})\otimes_{R}\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}})\to\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}})
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.5.6
, we have an isomorphism

\mu_{\mathcal{F},s}:\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F})\otimes_{R}\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}})\cong\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F})\otimes_{R^{s}}R(1)\cong\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}).

It remains to show that
\mu_{\mathcal{F},s}
agrees with
c_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F},\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}})
.
By Lemma
4.5.5
, it suffices to show this for
s\in S
a simple reflection.

The proof then follows from the same argument given in
[
LY20
, Lemma 7.7]
after the obvious replacements are made.
∎

Proposition 4.5.8
.

Let
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\in{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\mathcal{D}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ,\Theta}(\mathbbm{k})
.
The map
c_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F})
is an isomorphism if either
\mathcal{F}
or
\mathcal{G}
is a parity sheaf.
As a result, the restriction of the Soergel functor

\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}:{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\textnormal{Par}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ,\Theta}(\mathbbm{k})\to R_{\mathbbm{k}}\otimes R_{\mathbbm{k}}\textnormal{-mod}^{\mathbb{Z}}

is monoidal.

Proof.

Since the statement is symmetric, we may assume that
\mathcal{G}
is a parity sheaf. It suffices to prove the isomorphism for
\mathcal{G}=\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}
for
w\in W_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}^{\circ}
.
We can then find a reduced expression
\underline{w}=s_{1}\ldots s_{k}
of endosimple reflections in
W_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}^{\circ}
for
w
.
Note that
\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}
is a direct summand of
\IC_{s_{1}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\ldots\IC_{s_{k}}^{\mathcal{L}}
.
The proposition then follows from a successive application of Lemma
4.5.7

∎

4.6.
Comparison with Soergel Bimodules

We are ready to state the main result of the section.

Theorem 4.6.1
.

The
\mathbb{H}
-functor restricts to a monoidal equivalence of categories

\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}:{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\textnormal{Par}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ,\Theta}(\mathbbm{k})\to\mathbb{S}\textnormal{Bim}_{W_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}^{\circ}}(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbbm{k}}^{*})

such that
\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}})\cong B_{w}
for all
w\in W_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}^{\circ}
.

Note that by Proposition
4.5.8
and Lemma
4.5.6
,

\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}:{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\textnormal{Par}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ,\Theta}(\mathbbm{k})\to R_{\mathbbm{k}}\otimes R_{\mathbbm{k}}\textnormal{-mod}^{\mathbb{Z}}

is monoidal and satisfies
\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}})\cong B_{s}
for all
s\in S_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}
.
These observations together imply that
\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}})\cong B_{w}
for all
w\in W_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}
.
As a result, to prove Theorem
4.6.1
, it suffices to show that
\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}
is fully faithful.
Before proving full faithfulness, we need some preliminary lemmas.

Lemma 4.6.2
.

Let
\mathcal{G}\in{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\textnormal{Par}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ,\Theta}
. The natural map

\Hom^{\bullet}(\IC_{e}^{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{G})\to\Hom^{\bullet}(R,\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G}))

induced by
\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}
is an isomorphism of graded
R
-bimodules.

Proof.

The lemma follows from the proof of
[
LY20
, Lemma 7.10]
after the obvious modifications are made.
∎

Lemma 4.6.3
.

Let
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\in{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\textnormal{Par}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ,\Theta}
. Let
s\in\Theta
. There is a natural commutative diagram

(4.6.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hom^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{G})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hom^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hom^{\bullet}(\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}),\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hom^{\bullet}(\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F}),\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hom^{\bullet}(\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F})\otimes_{R}B_{s},\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hom^{\bullet}(\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F}),\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G})\otimes_{R}B_{s})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\circ c_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F},\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G},\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}})^{-1}\circ(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where the horizontal maps are given by the self-adjunction of
(-)\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}
and
(-)\otimes_{R}B_{s}
(Corollary
3.3.9
).

Proof.

We can factor the diagram (
4.6.1
) as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hom^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{G})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hom^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{G}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hom^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hom^{\bullet}(\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}),\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hom^{\bullet}(\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}),\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hom^{\bullet}(\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F}),\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hom^{\bullet}(\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F})\otimes_{R}B_{s},\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hom^{\bullet}(\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F})\otimes_{R}B_{s}\otimes_{R}B_{s},\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G})\otimes_{R}B_{s})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hom^{\bullet}(\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F}),\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G})\otimes_{R}B_{s})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\circ c_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F},\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(-\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G},\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}})^{-1}\circ(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\otimes_{R}B_{s}}

where the horizontal morphisms on the right are given by unit morphisms of adjunctions.
The top two squares commute by functorality of
\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}
. The bottom two squares commute by Proposition
4.5.8
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.6.1
.

Consider the morphism

\alpha(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}):\Hom^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})\to\Hom^{\bullet}(\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F}),\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G}))

Since every parity sheaf in
{}_{\mathcal{L}\backslash}\mathcal{D}_{/\mathcal{L}}^{\circ,\Theta}(\mathbbm{k})
is a direct sum of shifts of
\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}
for
w\in W_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}^{\circ}
it suffices to
prove that
\alpha(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
is an isomorphism for
\mathcal{F}=\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}
.
Let
\underline{w}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{k})
be a reduced expression for
w
in
W_{\mathcal{L},\Theta}^{\circ}
.
Furthermore, since
\IC_{w}^{\mathcal{L}}
is a direct summand of
\IC_{\underline{w}}^{\mathcal{L}}=\IC_{s_{1}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\ldots\star\IC_{s_{k}}^{\mathcal{L}}
, we can further
replace
\mathcal{F}=\IC_{\underline{w}}^{\mathcal{L}}
.
We will prove
\alpha(\IC_{\underline{w}}^{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{G})
is an isomorphism by induction on
\ell_{\mathcal{L}}(w)
. The base case of
w=e
is covered by Lemma

4.6.2
.

Let
\underline{w}^{\prime}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{k-1})
and write
s=s_{k}
. Note that
\underline{w}^{\prime}
is a reduced expression for
w^{\prime}=ws
.
Note that
\IC_{\underline{w}}^{\mathcal{L}}\cong\IC_{\underline{w}^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}
.
By Lemma
4.6.3
, we get a commutative diagram

(4.6.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hom^{\bullet}(\IC_{\underline{w}^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{G})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hom^{\bullet}(\IC_{\underline{w}^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{G}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hom^{\bullet}(\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\IC_{\underline{w}^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{L}}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}),\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hom^{\bullet}(\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\IC_{\underline{w}^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{L}}),\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G}\star\IC_{s}^{\mathcal{L}}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hom^{\bullet}(\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\IC_{\underline{w}^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{L}})\otimes_{R}B_{s},\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Hom^{\bullet}(\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\IC_{\underline{w}^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{L}}),\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G})\otimes_{R}B_{s})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
4.5.8
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
4.5.8
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

The top right vertical map is an isomorphism by induction; therefore, we must have that

\Hom^{\bullet}(\IC_{\underline{w}}^{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{G})\to\Hom^{\bullet}(\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\IC_{\underline{w}}^{\mathcal{L}}),\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G}))

is an isomorphism. We can then conclude that
\mathbb{H}_{\Theta}^{\mathcal{L}}
is fully faithful.

\square

5.
Endoscopic-Monodromic Equivalences

5.1.
Coefficient Rings

Let
\mathbbm{k}
be a noetherian ring of finite global dimension which admits a ring homomorphism
\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}\to\mathbbm{k}
.
extension of scalars along a ring map
\mathbbm{k}\to\mathbbm{k}^{\prime}
introduces two potential problems

(1)

If
\mathcal{L}\in\Ch(T,\mathbbm{k})
, then the endoscopic root system
\Phi_{\mathcal{L}}
could be strictly contained in
\Phi_{\mathbbm{k}^{\prime}(\mathcal{L})}
.

(2)

If
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbbm{k}^{\prime}}\in\Ch(T,\mathbbm{k}^{\prime})
, then there need not be a multiplicative local system
\mathcal{L}\in\Ch(T,\mathbbm{k})
such that
\mathbbm{k}^{\prime}(\mathcal{L})\cong\mathcal{L}_{\mathbbm{k}^{\prime}}
.

In general, we want to restrict to ring maps which preserve the endoscopic root system via extension of scalars.
Resolving the second problem is much more restrictive; however, we only need this to build an integral model of the Bott-Samelson category.
As a result, we do not need to require all of our ring maps to induce a surjective map on multiplicative local systems.

We give examples where problems (1) and (2) occur.

Example 5.1.1
.

Let
G=\SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

(1)

Let
\mathbbm{k}=\mathbb{C}[x^{\pm}]
and
\mathbbm{k}^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}
. Let
\varphi:\mathbb{C}[x^{\pm}]\to\mathbb{C}
be the
\mathbb{C}
-algebra map defined by
\varphi(x)=1
. Let
\gamma
denote a
generator of
\pi_{1}(T)
.
Let
\mathcal{L}\in\Ch(T,\mathbbm{k})
denote the local system corresponding to the
\pi_{1}(T)
-representation
\pi_{1}(T)\to\mathbbm{k}
defined by
\gamma\mapsto x
.
Then
\mathbbm{k}^{\prime}(\mathcal{L})
corresponds to the
\pi_{1}(T)
-representation
\pi_{1}(T)\to\mathbbm{k}^{\prime}
defined by
\gamma\mapsto 1
. Note that
\Phi_{\mathcal{L}}=\varnothing
whereas

\Phi_{\mathbbm{k}^{\prime}(\mathcal{L})}=\Phi
.

(2)

Consider the inclusion
\mathbb{Z}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Q}
. There is an identification
\Ch(T,\mathbb{Z})
with
\{1,-1\}
and
\Ch(T,\mathbb{Q})
with
\mathbb{Q}^{\times}
. As a result,
\mathbb{Q}(-):\Ch(T,\mathbb{Z})\to\Ch(T,\mathbb{Q})
is not surjective.

Definition 5.1.2
.

A
weak-Cohen lift of
\mathbbm{k}
is a ring
\mathscr{Z}
with ring homomorphism
\varphi:\mathscr{Z}\to\mathbbm{k}
such that

(1)

\mathscr{Z}
admits a ring homomorphism
\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{Z}
;

(2)

\mathscr{Z}
is a characteristic 0 noetherian domain of finite global dimension;

(3)

the group homomorphism
\varphi^{\times}:\mathscr{Z}^{\times}\to\mathbbm{k}^{\times}
admits a section.

Lemma 5.1.3
.

Let
\mathscr{Z}\to\mathbbm{k}
be a weak-Cohen lift. Then for every multiplicative local system
\mathcal{L}\in\Ch(T,\mathbbm{k})
, there exists a multiplicative local system
\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{Z}}\in\Ch(T,\mathscr{Z})
such that

\mathbbm{k}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{Z}})=\mathcal{L}
and
\Phi_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{Z}}}=\Phi_{\mathcal{L}}
.

Proof.

We can regard
\mathcal{L}
as a group homomorphism
\pi_{1}(T)\to\mathbbm{k}^{\times}
.
By composing this homomorphism with the section
\mathbbm{k}^{\times}\to\mathscr{Z}^{\times}
, we obtain a new multiplicative local system
\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{Z}}\in\Ch(T,\mathscr{Z})
.
Moreover,
\mathbbm{k}\otimes_{\mathscr{Z}}\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{Z}}
corresponds to the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{k}^{\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Z}^{\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{k}^{\times}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id}

which yields
\mathcal{L}
.

Let
\alpha^{\vee}\in\Phi^{\vee}
.
By a similar argument it can easily be checked that for an
(\alpha^{\vee})^{*}\mathcal{L}\cong\underline{\mathbbm{k}}_{\mathbb{G}_{m}}
if and only if
(\alpha^{\vee})^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{Z}}\cong\underline{\mathscr{Z}}_{\mathbb{G}_{m}}
.
As a result,
\alpha^{\vee}\in\Phi_{\mathcal{L}}^{\vee}
if and only if
\alpha^{\vee}\in\Phi_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathscr{Z}}}^{\vee}
.
∎

The condition that a weak-Cohen lift exist is rather forced. Nonetheless, it is a reasonably weak condition.

Example 5.1.4
.

(1)

Let
\mathbbm{k}
be a field. Then there exists a characteristic 0 complete noetherian DVR
A
whose residue field is
\mathbbm{k}
called a
Cohen ring
for
\mathbbm{k}

[
AJ22
]
.
The multiplicative section
\mathbbm{k}\to A
is given by Teichmüller representatives. As a result,
A
is weak-Cohen lift of
\mathbbm{k}
.

(2)

Let
\mathbbm{k}
be a perfect ring. Then the ring of
p
-typical Witt vectors is a weak-Cohen lift of
\mathbbm{k}

[
MB25
]
.

(3)

Let
\mathbbm{k}_{0}
be a perfect ring or a field. The polynomial ring
\mathbbm{k}=\mathbbm{k}_{0}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
admits a weak-Cohen lift. Similarly, any quotient or localization
of
\mathbbm{k}
at a prime ideal admits a weak-Cohen lift.

Definition 5.1.5
.

Let
\varphi:\mathbbm{k}\to\mathbbm{k}^{\prime}
be a morphism of rings. We say that
\varphi
is
unit-injective
if the induced group homomorphism
\varphi^{\times}:\mathbbm{k}^{\times}\to(\mathbbm{k}^{\prime})^{\times}
is injective.

Lemma 5.1.6
.

If
\varphi:\mathbbm{k}\to\mathbbm{k}^{\prime}
is unit-injective, then for all
\mathcal{L}\in\Ch(T,\mathbbm{k})
, there is an equality of root systems
\Phi_{\mathcal{L}}=\Phi_{\mathbbm{k}^{\prime}(\mathcal{L})}
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha^{\vee}\in\Phi
and let
\mathcal{L}\in\Ch(T,\mathbbm{k})
.
We regard
(\alpha^{\vee})^{*}\mathcal{L}
as a map
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{G}_{m})\to\mathbbm{k}^{\times}
. Similarly, we regard
(\alpha^{\vee})^{*}\mathbbm{k}^{\prime}(\mathcal{L})
as a map
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{G}_{m})\to(\mathbbm{k}^{\prime})^{\times}
.
Consider the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(\mathbb{G}_{m})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{k}^{\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbbm{k}^{\prime})^{\times}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha^{\vee})^{*}\mathcal{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha^{\vee})^{*}(\mathbbm{k}^{\prime}(\mathcal{L}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\times}}

Since
\varphi^{\times}
is injective, we have that
(\alpha^{\vee})^{*}\mathcal{L}\cong\underline{\mathbbm{k}}_{\mathbb{G}_{m}}
if and only if
(\alpha^{\vee})^{*}\mathbbm{k}^{\prime}(\mathcal{L})\cong\underline{\mathbbm{k}}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{G}_{m}}
.
∎

5.2.
Endoscopic Groups

We have seen that
(W_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ},S_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ})
is a Coxeter group with realization
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
.
We would like for
W_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}
to arise as the Weyl group of a Kac-Moody group with maximal torus
T
. This amounts to two conditions:

(1)

W_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}
is crystallographic;

(2)

the quadruple
(S_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ},X_{*}(T),\{\alpha_{s}\}_{s\in S_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}},\{\alpha_{s}^{\vee}\}_{s\in S_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}})
is a Kac-Moody root datum.

Condition (1) was shown to hold in
[
Héb23
, Lemma 3.5]
; however, it turns out that (2) does not always hold. In fact, when
W
is of indefinite type, then

W_{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}
can fail to be of finite rank.

Example 5.2.1

(
[
Héb23
, Lemma 3.11]
)
.

Consider the generalized Cartan matrix given as follows:

A=\begin{bmatrix}2&-2&-2&-2\\
-2&2&-2&-2\\
-2&-2&2&-2\\
-2&-2&-3&2\end{bmatrix}.

To
A
, we associate a Kac-Moody group
G
along with Borel
B
and maximal torus
T
. Let
\mathfrak{h}
denote the Lie algebra of
T
. Let
W=\langle s_{1},s_{2},s_{3},s_{4}\rangle
denote the Weyl group of
G
.
Note that
A
is invertible. As a result, we may assume that
\mathfrak{h}\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{4}\mathbb{C}\alpha_{i}^{\vee}
where the
\alpha_{i}^{\vee}
’s are the simple coroots.
Define a
\mathbb{C}
-linear map
f:\mathfrak{h}\to\mathbb{C}
by
h(\alpha_{i}^{\vee})=1
for all
i=1,2,3,4
.
We can then define a group homomorphism
\chi:X_{*}(T)\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
by
\chi(\phi)=(-1)^{f(\phi)}
.
Note that
\chi
corresponds with a multiplicative local system
\mathcal{L}_{\chi}\in\Ch(T,\mathbb{C})
.
Moreover, we have that
\alpha^{\vee}\in\Phi_{\re,\mathcal{L}_{\chi}}^{\vee}
if and only if
\chi(\alpha^{\vee})=1
.

Let
W^{\prime}=\langle s_{1},s_{2}\rangle\subset W
. We claim that for all
w\in W^{\prime}
that
ws_{3}\cdot\alpha_{4}^{\vee}\in\Phi_{\re,\mathcal{L}_{\chi}}^{\vee}
.
In order to see this, note that
s_{3}\cdot\alpha_{4}^{\vee}=\alpha_{4}^{\vee}+3\alpha_{3}^{\vee}
.
Moreover, if
i=1
or
i=2
, then
s_{i}\cdot(\alpha_{4}^{\vee}+3\alpha_{3}^{\vee}+2X_{*}(T))\subseteq(\alpha_{4}^{\vee}+3\alpha_{3}^{\vee}+2X_{*}(T))
.
As a result,
f(ws_{3}\cdot\alpha_{4}^{\vee})=1
and hence
ws_{3}\cdot\alpha_{4}^{\vee}\in\Phi_{\re,\mathcal{L}_{\chi}}^{\vee}
for all
w\in W^{\prime}
.

Next, we will show that
v=ws_{3}s_{4}s_{3}w^{-1}
, the reflection about
ws_{3}\cdot\alpha_{4}^{\vee}
, is endosimple.
For all
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
and
j\in\{1,2,3\}
, we have that
